💼 Goldman Sachs Small-Cap — 0 picks ▼
💼 Goldman Sachs Small-Cap
Small-cap hidden-gem scanner: $100M-$2B cap, 25%+ growth, thin coverage, insider ownership, moat, catalysts, red flags.
📖 Strategy details▼

Goldman Sachs pre-discovery screen — finding companies growing 25%+ in the $100M–$2B range before mainstream institutional flows arrive. Goal: tomorrow's $10B company at $500M today.

  • Size: $100M–$2B market cap — big enough to be legitimate, small enough to still multiply
  • Coverage: 0–5 analysts covering the stock (≥20 = already discovered)
  • Revenue growth: minimum 25% YoY for 3+ consecutive quarters
  • Insider ownership: founders/executives holding 15%+ of shares (skin in the game)
  • Structural tailwinds: AI, cybersecurity, energy transition, aging demographics, automation
  • Unit economics: improving gross margins and positive operating leverage (revenue growing faster than costs)
  • Balance sheet: 18+ months cash runway without needing profitability
  • Blindspot channels: spinoffs from S&P 500 companies, foreign listings overlooked by US investors, orphaned small-caps that fell below $500M and lost analyst coverage, post-bankruptcy equities with clean balance sheets
  • Near-term catalysts: specific re-rating events within 6–12 months (earnings beats, product launches, regulatory approvals)
🛒 Lynch Buy What You Know — 0 picks ▼
🛒 Lynch Buy What You Know
Everyday-observation → public-company mapping, classified into a Lynch category with a valuation sanity check.
📖 Strategy details▼

Peter Lynch's everyday-life observation framework. Simple, understandable consumer and workplace businesses growing 15–25%/yr at PEG <1.0. The best investments hide in plain sight — find them before Wall Street does.

  • Daily life inventory: products, apps, or services you're using more this year than last year
  • Emerging behaviour patterns: what friends, family, and colleagues are doing differently than 2 years ago
  • Workplace intelligence: what tools or software your company is buying or switching to
  • Kids' trends: products and brands teenagers are obsessed with that parents haven't noticed yet
  • Retail observation: which stores have lines, which brands are sold out, what everyone is talking about
  • Lynch category: classify each as Fast Grower (20%+ growth), Stalwart, Cyclical, Turnaround, or Asset Play
  • The 'so what' test: a popular product doesn't automatically make a great stock — always check valuation and fundamentals
📱 Camillo Social Arbitrage — 0 picks ▼
📱 Camillo Social Arbitrage
Viral TikTok/Reddit/X/App-Store signals mapped to the public company capturing the economic benefit.
📖 Strategy details▼

Chris Camillo's social arbitrage approach — spotting consumer trends on social media months before Wall Street analysts notice, finding what's viral before it shows up in earnings reports.

  • TikTok / Reddit / X: products and brands going viral with millions of views that analysts haven't quantified yet
  • Google Trends divergence: search terms spiking while stock prices haven't moved yet (early signal)
  • YouTube review velocity: products suddenly accumulating dozens of review videos with high engagement
  • App Store rankings: apps climbing free and paid charts that belong to investable companies
  • Amazon bestsellers: products climbing rankings that belong to public companies
  • Lead time: social signals typically precede earnings impact by 6–12 months — the arbitrage window
  • Public company linkage: for each trend, identify the specific public company that captures the economic benefit
💯 Mayer 100-Bagger — 0 picks ▼
💯 Mayer 100-Bagger
Small cap + high ROIC + owner-operator + long runway — the 100-bagger checklist. May return zero picks.
📖 Strategy details▼

Christopher Mayer's 100-Bagger framework — the research methodology identifying stocks with potential to return 100x, based on patterns observed across 365 historical 100-baggers.

  • Small starting cap: ideally under $1B — 100x must be mathematically possible
  • Long runway: serves a market large enough to support decades of revenue growth
  • ROIC above 20%: Mayer's single most predictive metric — the engine of compounding
  • Earnings growth: 20%+ annual earnings growth sustained for many years
  • Owner-operator: CEO and management with 10%+ personal stock ownership
  • Reinvestment runway: profits redeployed into new growth at high returns (not paid out as dividends)
  • No dilution: the company isn't issuing large amounts of new shares that erode long-term returns
  • Twin engines test: can earnings grow 10x AND the P/E multiple also expand to compound into 100x?
  • Time horizon reality: 100-baggers typically take 20–25 years — patience is the edge
🔢 Greenblatt Magic Formula — 0 picks ▼
🔢 Greenblatt Magic Formula
ROIC>20% + earnings yield>10% ranked screen, ex-financials/utilities/REITs. Top 10, not a curated shortlist.
📖 Strategy details▼

Joel Greenblatt's Magic Formula — the two-factor screen generating 30%+ annual returns over 20 years by finding companies that are simultaneously high-quality AND cheap.

  • ROIC above 20%: high-quality business generating strong returns on every dollar invested
  • Earnings yield above 10%: cheap relative to earnings (inverse of P/E) — the 'cheap' gate
  • Combined ranking: companies score best when BOTH metrics rank highly — quality + cheap together
  • Size filter: $50M–$10B to exclude illiquid micro-caps and already-covered mega-caps
  • Sector exclusions: skip financials, utilities, and REITs (different capital structures distort the formula)
  • Debt check: high ROIC shouldn't be mere financial leverage — verify balance sheet is clean
  • Earnings quality: free cash flow should match net income — flag accounting-driven earnings
  • Temporary vs permanent: is the stock cheap due to a fixable problem, or is the business structurally declining?
🎲 Pabrai Asymmetric Bet — 0 picks ▼
🎲 Pabrai Asymmetric Bet
3:1+ upside/downside bets with explicit downside protection — distress, special situations, hated industries.
📖 Strategy details▼

Mohnish Pabrai's Dhandho framework — combining LOW RISK (limited downside) with HIGH UNCERTAINTY (Wall Street can't value it) for asymmetric bets where heads I win big, tails I don't lose much.

  • Asymmetry ratio: upside potential ÷ downside risk must be 3:1 or better
  • Downside protection: what specifically limits the loss — asset value, cash, acquisition target, recurring revenue
  • Upside catalyst: the specific event that could drive 3–10x returns over 3–5 years
  • Temporary distress: markets treating a short-term problem (lawsuit, earnings miss, sector rotation) as permanent
  • Special situations: spinoffs, post-bankruptcy equities, rights offerings that create pricing inefficiencies
  • Hated industries: entire sectors abandoned by investors where survivors trade at distressed multiples
  • Margin of safety: gap between current price and conservative intrinsic value — the buffer against being wrong
🔄 Marks Second-Level — 0 picks ▼
🔄 Marks Second-Level
Consensus audit + second-level question — oversold/overbought extremes, narrative-vs-numbers gaps.
📖 Strategy details▼

Howard Marks' second-level thinking — first-level thinking gets average returns; second-level thinking ('the company is great BUT priced for perfection') finds the real opportunities.

  • Consensus audit: what is the mainstream view, and is it based on sound analysis or herd mentality?
  • Second-level question: what do I know or see that the consensus is missing?
  • Oversold opportunities: stocks down 50%+ where the negative narrative has become excessive vs fundamentals
  • Sentiment extremes: sectors at multi-year sentiment lows (rare to be wrong buying) or highs (rare to be right buying)
  • Narrative vs numbers gap: where does the market story ('this industry is dying') contradict the actual data?
  • Time horizon arbitrage: quarterly-focused Wall Street missing a multi-year thesis
  • Insider buying signal: executives aggressively buying when the market is selling — the strongest bullish insider indicator
🌀 Sleep Scale Economics Shared — 0 picks ▼
🌀 Sleep Scale Economics Shared
Businesses that share scale economics with customers via a reinforcing flywheel and long-term management.
📖 Strategy details▼

Nick Sleep's Scale Economics Shared framework — the methodology that identified Amazon, Costco, and Berkshire decades early, based on companies that share their scale advantages with customers to build a self-reinforcing flywheel.

  • Core test: does the company get MORE efficient and offer BETTER prices as it grows? (opposite of most businesses)
  • Customer obsession: willing to take short-term margin hits to give customers more value (Costco 15% margin discipline)
  • Flywheel: lower prices → more customers → more scale → lower costs → even lower prices (self-reinforcing loop)
  • Long-term thinking evidence: quarterly earnings suppressed for multi-year investment; founder letters emphasise decades
  • Customer lifetime value: expanding over time as the business creates more value per customer
  • Non-obvious moat: the advantage isn't patents or brand — it's a culture and business model the organisation embodies
  • Capital allocation: cash flow reinvested into high-return opportunities rather than buybacks at peak prices
🕳️ Burry Catalyst Deep Value — 0 picks ▼
🕳️ Burry Catalyst Deep Value
Hidden assets, sum-of-the-parts, activist/regulatory catalysts — each with an explicit timeline.
📖 Strategy details▼

Michael Burry's catalyst-driven deep value approach — finding mispriced assets where specific, identifiable catalysts will force the market to recognise hidden value within a defined timeframe.

  • Hidden assets: companies trading below the value of real estate, cash, or investments on the balance sheet
  • Sum-of-parts: conglomerates where individual divisions are worth more than the combined market cap
  • Activist catalyst: stocks where an activist could force a spinoff, sale, or restructuring to unlock value
  • Regulatory / legal catalysts: upcoming court cases or policy decisions that will materially reprice the stock
  • Balance sheet transformation: companies paying down debt aggressively — will re-rate once leverage drops below key thresholds
  • Accounting normalisation: one-time write-downs or unusual items temporarily depressing reported earnings
  • Catalyst timeline: each idea must have a specific catalyst with 6–18 month resolution — no 'cheap forever' open-ended theses
🛰️ Wall Street Blindspot — 0 picks ▼
🛰️ Wall Street Blindspot
Coverage-gap hunting — foreign listings, spinoffs, orphaned small-caps, sin-stock discounts — as a prioritized workflow.
📖 Strategy details▼

Wall Street Blindspot framework — finding investment opportunities that major banks structurally cannot or will not cover: coverage gaps, orphaned names, stigmatised sectors, complex structures.

  • Coverage gap: companies with 0–5 sell-side analysts — institutions only cover above $2B, leaving quality names persistently mispriced
  • Orphaned mid-caps: companies that fell below $500M and lost coverage — often trading at deep discounts to intrinsic value
  • Spinoff aftermath: newly independent companies facing forced selling from index funds in the first 18 months
  • Sin-sector discounts: quality companies in gambling, tobacco, fossil fuels, or defence at persistent ESG mandate discounts
  • Post-bankruptcy emergence: restructured balance sheets institutional investors can't own due to mandate restrictions
  • IPO aftermath: quality companies 18–24 months post-IPO when lockup expiry creates selling pressure
  • Key signal: the discount reason is structural or temporary, not fundamental — the business is better than the price suggests
